<div id="anp_a" class="d-panel p" style="display: none"><div class="notes p"><section id="historyarticle-meta1" class="history"><p>Received 2024 Sep 16; Accepted 2025 Jan 16; Revised 2025 Jan 15; Collection date 2025 Feb 11.</p></section></div></div>

<p>Permits the broadest form of re-use including for commercial purposes, provided that author attribution and integrity are maintained (<a href="https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://creativecommons.org/licenses/by/4.0/</a>).</p>

</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/5cf66c1da8e7/es4c09615_0008.jpg" loading="lazy" id="ab-tgr1" height="344" width="661" alt="graphic file with name es4c09615_0008.jpg"></p>
<p>The complex and dynamic
nature of airborne fine particulate matter
(PM<sub>2.5</sub>) has hindered understanding of its chemical composition,
sources, and toxic effects. In the first steps of a larger study,
here, we aimed to elucidate relationships between source regions,
ambient conditions, and the chemical composition in water extracts
of PM<sub>2.5</sub> samples (<em>n</em> = 85) collected over
16 months at an observatory in the Yellow Sea. In each extract, we
quantified elements and major ions and profiled the complex mixtures
of organic compounds by nontarget mass spectrometry. More than 50,000
nontarget features were detected, and by consensus of in silico tools,
we assigned a molecular formula to 13,907 features. Oxygenated compounds
were most prominent, followed by mixed nitrogenated/oxygenated compounds,
organic sulfates, and sulfonates. Spectral matching enabled identification
or structural annotation of 43 substances, and a workflow involving
SIRIUS and MS-DIAL software enabled annotation of 74 unknown per-
and polyfluoroalkyl substances with primary source regions in China
and the Korean Peninsula. Multivariate modeling revealed seasonal
variations in chemistry, attributable to the combination of warmer
temperatures and maritime source regions in summer and to cooler temperatures
and source regions of China in winter.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> PM<sub>2.5</sub>, air pollution, high-resolution
mass spectrometry, nontarget, sources, PFAS</p></section></section><section class="abstract" id="abstract2"><h2>Short abstract</h2>
<p>The water-soluble
fraction of airborne fine particulates
was comprehensively characterized over 16 months to better understand
the chemical nature and sources of organic and inorganic substances
contributing to air pollution in east Asian regions.</p></section><section id="sec1"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Introduction</h2>
<p>Exposure to airborne fine particulate
matter (i.e., PM<sub>2.5</sub>; diameter &lt;2.5 μm) has been
linked to early death, cancer,
stroke, and chronic lung and heart disease.<sup><a href="#ref1" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref1">1</a>,<a href="#ref2" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref2">2</a></sup> PM<sub>2.5</sub> can be deposited deep in the lungs, and the ultrafine fraction
(i.e., &lt;0.1 μm) can even cross biological barriers to enter
the bloodstream,<sup><a href="#ref3" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref3">3</a></sup> making PM<sub>2.5</sub> a source of exposure to physical particulates and its entrained
chemical load.<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a></sup> A large fraction of PM<sub>2.5</sub> by mass is highly water-soluble, an important factor affecting
exposure through inhalation.<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a></sup> Although
a global problem, some of the highest levels of PM<sub>2.5</sub> are
reported in regions of the Asian continent.<sup><a href="#ref5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref5">5</a></sup> Prior to the global pandemic, in 2019, people in China and South
Korea were exposed to PM<sub>2.5</sub> levels exceeding the World
Health Organization’s air quality guideline limit (15 μg/m<sup>3</sup>) on most days of the year (336 days in China, 319 days in
South Korea).<sup><a href="#ref5" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref5">5</a></sup> Some prevention programs
have been implemented; for example, in China, the Air Pollution Prevention
and Control Action Plan helped to reduce PM<sub>2.5</sub> between
2013 and 2017, highlighting the need for stronger sustainable efforts.<sup><a href="#ref6" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref6">6</a></sup> Similar emission control policies in South Korea
helped to reduce PM<sub>2.5</sub> pollution by 9% between 2016 and
2020.<sup><a href="#ref7" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref7">7</a></sup> Additionally, since 2020, a management
system has been implemented to reduce seasonal emissions of PM<sub>2.5</sub>.<sup><a href="#ref8" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref8">8</a></sup></p>
<p>The chemical composition
of PM<sub>2.5</sub> includes a complex
and dynamic mixture of organic substances, as well as inorganic ions,
trace elements, and black carbon.<sup><a href="#ref9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref9">9</a>−<a href="#ref11" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref11">11</a></sup> Advancements in high-resolution
mass spectrometry (HRMS) methods are now allowing wider targeted analysis
and nontarget profiling for identification and broad molecular characterization
of organic substances in PM<sub>2.5</sub>.<sup><a href="#ref9" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref9">9</a>,<a href="#ref12" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref12">12</a>−<a href="#ref17" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref17">17</a></sup> In previous work, we applied nontarget HRMS to PM<sub>2.5</sub> emitted
from regions of South Asia and demonstrated that the complexity of
organic substances was greatest in the water-soluble fraction, compared
to polar and nonpolar solvent extracts.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> The water-soluble fraction of PM<sub>2.5</sub> has been identified
as a contributor to toxicity, including as the main contributor to
reactive oxygen species generation in rat cells<sup><a href="#ref18" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref18">18</a></sup> and in the acellular dithiothreitol (DTT) assay, where
the important role of water-soluble organic compounds (WSOCs) has
also been demonstrated.<sup><a href="#ref19" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref19">19</a></sup> Moreover, in
aqueous extracts of PM<sub>2.5</sub> from China, Di et al. observed
a correlation between the total concentration of WSOCs and apoptosis
in exposed human lung cells.<sup><a href="#ref20" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref20">20</a></sup> The ecotoxicity
of the water-soluble fraction of PM<sub>2.5</sub> is also relevant,
given its demonstrated toxicity to green algae.<sup><a href="#ref21" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref21">21</a></sup> Compared to hydrophobic substances, water-soluble components
of PM<sub>2.5</sub> are more likely to be bioavailable and interact
rapidly with biological systems when inhaled,<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a>,<a href="#ref22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref22">22</a></sup> and
a previous study has shown that the water-soluble fraction is also
the most chemically diverse.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> However,
the limited number of studies characterizing WSOCs from PM<sub>2.5</sub> poses a challenge to understanding its chemical composition and
toxicity. Therefore, this study proposes to characterize both inorganic
and organic components, moving beyond the suspect screening of WSOCs,
and aims to understand the critical changes in water-extracted PM<sub>2.5</sub> depending on the sources and atmospheric conditions.</p>
<p>For the present study, we collected PM<sub>2.5</sub> by a high-volume
air sampler (48–72 h) between November 2019 and March 2021
(a total of 85 samples) at the Gosan Climate Observatory (Jeju Island,
Republic of Korea). Aqueous extracts of PM<sub>2.5</sub> are cytotoxic
to primary human cells. Thus, the primary study objective was to comprehensively
characterize inorganic and organic substances in each of these toxicologically
relevant water-soluble fractions. A series of chemical analyses were
conducted on each extract by employing nontarget HRMS for organic
substances, inductively coupled plasma (ICP)-MS for metals and metalloids,
and ion chromatography for major ions. A secondary objective was to
use multivariate tools to understand how the complex chemical compositions
changed with the back-trajectory source and atmospheric conditions.
Overall, the chemical complexity and seasonal variability of these
toxic samples are presented; hazardous anthropogenic pollutants are
discovered; and associations between PM<sub>2.5</sub> chemistry, meteorology,
and air–mass origins are elucidated. These results will inform
future work on the molecular drivers of toxicity in PM<sub>2.5</sub>.</p></section><section id="sec2"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Materials and Methods</h2>
<section id="sec2.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Sample Collection and Handling</h3>
<p>Ambient
monitoring and
sample collection were carried out at the Gosan Climate Observatory
(33.2933°N, 126.1625°E, 72 m above mean sea level), located
in the Yellow Sea on Jeju Island, Republic of Korea. Due to its location
in the path of aerosols transported from the Asian continent,<sup><a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">23</a></sup> it is a strategic receptor site for monitoring
air pollution originating from China, Japan, and the Korean Peninsula.<sup><a href="#ref10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref10">10</a>,<a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">24</a></sup> Sampling of airborne fine particulates was performed by using a
high-volume sampler with a PM<sub>2.5</sub> selective inlet (sampler
model: DH-77, Digitel Elektronik AG, Volketswil, Switzerland). PM<sub>2.5</sub> samples were collected on in-line quartz filters (150 mm
⌀) prepared from quartz fiber membranes (Merck Millipore, France)
and prefurnaced at 450 °C for 6 h in individual aluminum envelopes.
Filter presampling weights were recorded under controlled temperature
(20 °C) and humidity (50%) after 24 h of equilibration. The filters
were shipped in individually sealed aluminum envelopes and sealed
bags to the observatory. Each filter was exposed for 48–72
h at a calibrated flow of 500 L/min, typically three filters per week,
plus a field blank. Samples were kept frozen (−20 °C)
and shipped to the laboratory in Stockholm, where postweights were
recorded in the same climate-controlled room as preweights. In total,
102 filters were collected, but due to high winds and rain during
collection, 15 filters were noted to be wet during collection and
were therefore excluded, leaving 85 PM<sub>2.5</sub> samples for analysis
here, along with 30 field blanks.</p></section><section id="sec2.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Back-Trajectory Calculations
and Clustering</h3>
<p>For the
sampling period of each sample, 48 h back-trajectories of the air
reaching the observatory were calculated every hour using the Hybrid
Single-Particle Lagrangian Integrated Trajectory model (HYSPLIT, v.
5.2.0<sup><a href="#ref25" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref25">25</a></sup>) with 3 h data from the National
Centers for Environmental Prediction (NCEP) Global Data Assimilation
System (GDAS) as input meteorological fields.<sup><a href="#ref26" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref26">26</a></sup> The Fuzzy c-mean clustering method<sup><a href="#ref27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref27">27</a></sup> was
used to classify the air–mass trajectories into six source
regions, chosen as the optimal clustering result according to criteria
outlined previously,<sup><a href="#ref28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref28">28</a>,<a href="#ref29" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref29">29</a></sup> resulting in 54.5% of back-trajectories
being clustered to one of the six source regions. Each source region
was numbered and named according to air–mass origins (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>A): 1—East
Sea/Sea of Japan, 2—East China Sea, 3—Korean Peninsula,
4—Shandong/Beijing, 5—North China, and 6—North
China/Mongolia.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig1"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/820234f099ba/es4c09615_0001.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr1" height="4827" width="3417" alt="Figure 1"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig1/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(A) Over the campaign, backward trajectories (48 h) of
sampled
air calculated every hour clustered to six source regions: 1—East
Sea/Sea of Japan; 2—East China Sea; 3—Korean Peninsula;
4—Shandong/Beijing; 5—North China, 6—North China/Mongolia.
For each sample (<em>n</em> = 85), the (B) relative contribution
(%) of air coming from each source region and (C) average air temperatures
(°C) and PM<sub>2.5</sub> concentrations (μg/m<sup>3</sup>, gravimetric on filters) are shown. The WHO air quality guideline
limit is also shown as a dashed line (15 μg/m<sup>3</sup>).
The time axes of (B) and (C) are not continuous due to missing or
excluded samples because of storms.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Aqueous Extraction of PM<sub>2.5</sub>
</h3>
<p>From all filters
in the sampling period, WSOCs were extracted using the same protocol
as Papazian et al.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> Briefly, half of each
filter was cut using clean scissors and tweezers, weighed in a controlled
climate room, and placed in a centrifuge tube (high-grade γ-resistant
polypropylene, VWR International AB, Sweden) for extraction with 20
mL of high-performance liquid chromatography (HPLC)-grade water (Honeywell
AB, Bromma, Sweden). The tubes were then sonicated at room temperature
for 30 min and centrifuged for 10 min at 1800<em>g</em>. The
resulting supernatant was recovered and filtered with 0.2 μm
cellulose syringe filters (Minisart RC 15 mm, Sartorius AG, Göttingen,
Germany), and eight internal standards were spiked in all of the extracts
at 0.23 μg/mL each (phenylglyoxylic acid-<em>d</em><sub>5</sub>, daidzein-<em>d</em><sub>6</sub>, <em>N</em>-acetyl-<em>d</em><sub>3</sub>-<em>S</em>-(<em>N</em>-methylcarbamoyl)-<span class="font-variant-small-caps">l</span>-cysteine, atrazine-2-hydroxy-<em>d</em><sub>3</sub>, norharmane-<em>d</em><sub>7</sub>, atrazine-<em>d</em><sub>5</sub>, 4-(methylnitrosamino)-1-(3-pyridyl)-1-butanol-<em>d</em><sub>5</sub>, and racemic enterolactone-<sup>13</sup>C<sub>3</sub>). The internal standards were not added before extraction
because a portion of each extract was used for toxicity tests. The
aqueous extracts were stored at −20 °C before chemical
and toxicological analyses.</p></section><section id="sec2.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Nontarget HRMS Analysis</h3>
<p>The water
extracts were analyzed
by in-line solid-phase extraction (SPE)–liquid chromatography
(LC)–HRMS, following the same protocol and instrumental setup
as Papazian et al.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> Briefly, 1.05 mL of
aqueous extracts was injected into two in-line SPE columns in series:
polar end-capped C18 (Thermo Scientific Hypersil Gold aQ, 2.1 mm ×
20 mm, 12 μm particle) followed by pentafluorophenyl (Thermo
Scientific Hypersil Gold PFP, 2.1 mm × 20 mm, 12 μm particle).
The SPE columns were then reverse-flow-eluted by the mobile-phase
gradient onto an analytical C18 UPLC column (Waters Acquity UPLC BEH
C18, 3.0 × 100 mm<sup>2</sup>, 1.7 μm particle) with a
0.2 μm in-line filter in a column oven at 40 °C. The gradient
flow was 0.4 mL/min and composed of water containing 0.1% formic acid
(mobile phase A) and methanol containing 0.1% formic acid (mobile
phase B). The initial conditions were 5% B, increasing to 95% after
24 min before returning to initial conditions for 4 min of equilibration.</p>
<p>The chromatographic setup was coupled to a quadrupole orbitrap
mass spectrometer (Q Exactive Orbitrap HF-X, Thermo Fisher) and operated
in electrospray ionization (ESI) mode, with separate injections performed
for positive and negative ESI modes. The capillary voltage was +3.0
or −2.5 kV, the capillary temperature was 300 °C, and
the auxiliary gas temperature was 400 °C. The sheath gas flow
was 50, the auxiliary gas flow was 16, and the sweep gas flow was
2 (arbitrary units). Spectral acquisition included full-scan (MS1,
90–1000 <em>m</em>/<em>z</em>) and data-independent
(MS2 DIA) MS/MS acquisition with four isolation windows (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 90–320, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 315–551, <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 542–778,
and <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 769–1000) and dual
collision energies (20, 70 V). MS2 fragments from both collision energies
were analyzed simultaneously in the orbitrap with an auto-gain control
target of 10<sup>6</sup>.</p>
<p>Samples were injected in random order,
instrument blanks (HPLC
grade water) were injected after six sample injections, and a quality
control (QC) sample was also created by pooling 32 randomly selected
extracts, injected at the beginning and end of the injection sequence
for both ionization modes. A retention time index (RTI) solution developed
for nontarget screening<sup><a href="#ref30" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref30">30</a>,<a href="#ref31" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref31">31</a></sup> was also injected on
each day to aid structural annotations. Mass calibration was performed
externally prior to each batch run (all of the injections were divided
in seven batches) using the Pierce ion calibration solution (Thermo
Scientific) for each mode.</p></section><section id="sec2.5"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">HRMS Data Processing</h3>
<p>Software MS-DIAL
(v. 3.78) was
used to process raw data from nontarget HRMS acquisition,<sup><a href="#ref32" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref32">32</a></sup> including for deconvolution of MS2 spectra.
Injections of the RTI solutions were used to optimize MS-DIAL parameters,
and the detected reference peaks in each mode (13 and 14, in positive
and negative ionization modes, respectively) were manually checked
to ensure that the peaks were correctly integrated and aligned and
that MS2 spectra were accurately deconvoluted before proceeding (see
the optimized parameters in <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S1</a>).
The field blanks were set as “blank” in MS-DIAL, and
nontarget features in samples were only kept if their peak intensity
was &gt;5 times the field blank average. To correct all nontarget
feature
peak areas for instrumental drift or batch variation, integrated peak
areas of the internal standards in all samples and field blanks were
examined by principal component analysis (PCA), with unit variance
no centering (UVN) scaling (SIMCA 17, Sartorius Stedim Data Analytics
AB, Sweden). For results in each ionization mode, all peak areas in
each sample were then normalized to internal standards by dividing
peak areas with the corresponding PCA <em>t</em>1 score.<sup><a href="#ref33" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref33">33</a></sup> To evaluate the impact of the back-trajectories
on a feature’s intensity, the peak areas of the features were
normalized between 0 and 1 throughout the data set, and the scaled
area of the feature in all samples was multiplied by the source region
cluster weight on the corresponding sampling days. For any chemical
or nontarget feature, the relative contribution (%) of each source
region cluster throughout the campaign was estimated by summing the
peak area and the corresponding back-trajectory cluster weights for
each sample, dividing it by the sum of all peak areas in all samples,
and multiplying it by the cluster weight of the feature.</p></section><section id="sec2.6"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Molecular Formula
Assignments</h3>
<p>The .mat files for all
of the detected features were exported from MS-DIAL. The .mat format
is compatible with other software and contains MS1 data, isotopic
ratios, and fragmentation spectra of each nontarget feature. Molecular
formula prediction of unknown features was performed using both SIRIUS
(v. 5.6.3)<sup><a href="#ref34" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref34">34</a></sup> and MS-FINDER (v. 3.52) software.<sup><a href="#ref35" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref35">35</a></sup> Parameters used in SIRIUS were as follows: instrument,
Orbitrap; MS2 mass accuracy, 3 ppm; isotopic score, SCORE; and database
formula sources, NORMAN and PubChem. CSI:FingerID (which looks for
structural fingerprints<sup><a href="#ref27" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref27">27</a></sup>) was enabled
in SIRIUS to aid in ranking the predicted molecular formulas.<sup><a href="#ref28" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref28">28</a></sup> Parameters used in MS-FINDER were the following:
mass tolerance (ms1 and ms2), 3 ppm; formula finder checks, LEWIS
and SENIOR rules and element probability check; element selection,
O, N, P, S, F, Cl, Br, and I; data sources, T3DB and STOFF (environment);
and use PubChem online when no query matches are found from a local
database. The molecular formulas ranked first and second by both software
were exported. When agreement between the two software tools was found,
defined here as the same formula suggested by both <em>in silico</em> fragmentation tools within their top two candidates, the molecular
formula was assigned. For further quality control, if the assigned
formula contained Cl, the isotope ratio was manually checked, and
the formula was only retained if the M + 2 isotope was also present.</p></section><section id="sec2.7"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">High-Resolution MS2 Spectral Library Searches</h3>
<p>MassBank
Europe and MassBank of North America (MoNA) were both downloaded in
February 2021 (<a href="https://massbank.eu/MassBank/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://massbank.eu/MassBank/</a>; <a href="https://mona.fiehnlab.ucdavis.edu/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://mona.fiehnlab.ucdavis.edu/</a>). The MSP files were used in MS-DIAL for spectral matching using
an MS2 similarity score of at least 70%, MS1 tolerance of 0.01 Da,
and MS2 tolerance of 0.015 Da. All initial annotations were then checked
manually and discarded if they were deemed insufficient by visual
inspection of the spectra. For the remaining annotated features, RTI
was then calculated using the dedicated website at <a href="http://rti.chem.uoa.gr/" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">http://rti.chem.uoa.gr/</a>. From
the website, the equation given by the observed retention times of
the RTI standards, injected with the samples, was retrieved and used
to calculate feature RTIs (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3</a>).
The predicted RTI of the candidate structure was retrieved from the
online Norman Substance Database (<a href="https://www.norman-network.com/nds/susdat/susdatSearchShow.php" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">https://www.norman-network.com/nds/susdat/susdatSearchShow.php</a>). Finally, only those annotations with ΔRTI lower than or
equal to 30 were kept, and the molecular structures were assigned
with level 2 confidence according to the criteria of Schymanski et
al.<sup><a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup> When possible, authentic standards
were obtained and injected following the same instrumental setup,
and their measured RTIs, precursor masses, isotopic ratios, and MS2
spectra were compared to the annotated feature to potentially confirm
the structure at level 1 confidence.</p></section><section id="sec2.8"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Analysis of Trace Metals
and Transition Elements</h3>
<p>An
eighth of each sample filter was separately extracted in 10 mL of
HPLC-grade water following the protocol described above, followed
by sonication, centrifugation, and filtering. The new extracts were
analyzed by inductively coupled plasma-mass spectrometry (ICP-MS;
iCAP Qc, Thermo Fisher Scientific, Germany). Quantification was by
calibration curves (Al, Ti, Cr, Mn, Fe, Co, Ni, Cu, Zn, Ga, Sr, Ag,
Ca, Ba, Tl, Pb, and B) normalized to the internal standard response
(In and Sc). All standards were Certipur-grade (Merck). Kinetic energy
discrimination mode, using 99.999% helium gas, was applied to the
selected compounds. Instrument detection limits were between 0.1 and
10 ppb.</p></section><section id="sec2.9"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Analysis of Major Ions</h3>
<p>Inorganic constituents were
measured by ion chromatography (IC, Dionex Aquion, Thermo Finnigan
LLC, Walkersville, United States) in the same aqueous extracts as
for nontarget LC-HRMS. The 11 major ions routinely detected in PM<sub>2.5</sub> were quantified by calibrations curves between 0.1 and
100 mg/L, including 6 anions (chloride, nitrite, bromide, nitrate,
phosphate, sulfate) and 5 cations (sodium, ammonium, potassium, magnesium,
calcium).<sup><a href="#ref10" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref10">10</a>,<a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a></sup> As a quality control, two reference solutions
with known concentrations of chloride, sulfate, sodium, and potassium
(i.e., two anions and two cations) were also injected before the samples
on each day of analysis. Signals of these reference samples were stable,
with a relative standard deviation for all ions in the range of 0.2–1.5%.
When necessary, the concentrations in sample extracts were corrected
by subtracting the highest concentration detected in field blanks.</p></section><section id="sec2.10"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Monitoring of Ambient Pollutants at the Gosan Climate Observatory</h3>
<p>The ambient ground-level concentrations of gaseous pollutants (i.e.,
SO<sub>2</sub>, CO, O<sub>3</sub>, NO<sub>2</sub>) and the mass concentration
of particulate matter (i.e., PM<sub>2.5</sub>, PM<sub>10</sub>) were
measured simultaneously on an hourly basis by an air quality monitoring
station (AQMS), processed under the framework of the National Ambient
air quality Monitoring Information System (NAMIS) in South Korea.<sup><a href="#ref23" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref23">23</a></sup> Hourly PM<sub>10</sub> and PM<sub>2.5</sub> mass
concentrations were measured using the filter-based β-ray absorption
method (BAM-1020; Met One Instruments Inc.). Volume mixing ratios
of SO<sub>2</sub>, CO, O<sub>3</sub>, and NO<sub>2</sub> were measured
with instruments manufactured by Teledyne API using the following
technologies: pulsed UV fluorescence (SO<sub>2</sub>), gas filter
correlation (CO), UV photometry (O<sub>3</sub>), and chemiluminescence
(NO<sub>2</sub>).</p></section><section id="sec2.11"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Statistical Modeling</h3>
<p>Principal components
analysis
(PCA), orthogonal partial least-squares discriminant analysis (OPLS-DA),
and <em>T</em>-distributed stochastic neighbor embedding (<em>t</em>-sne) analysis were carried out following the protocol
described in the <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Supporting Information</a> (p. S3).</p></section><section id="sec2.12"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Human Nasal Cell Viability Assay</h3>
<p>The assay used to
assess the viability of human primary nasal epithelial cells exposed
to PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracts is described in detail in the <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Supporting Information</a> (p. S2).</p></section></section><section id="sec3"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Results
and Discussion</h2>
<p>The back-trajectories and measured ambient
parameters (temperature,
PM<sub>2.5</sub> mass, and ambient gas concentrations) over the entire
sampling period are summarized in <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figures <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a> and <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">S1</a> and discussed
in the <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Supporting Information</a> (p. S4).
Briefly, the air–mass back-trajectories and their seasonal
frequencies (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>A,B) are similar to previously reported observations at the Gosan
Climate Observatory,<sup><a href="#ref38" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref38">38</a></sup> and the PM<sub>2.5</sub> concentrations (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>C) are lower than what Kim et al. observed on Jeju Island
between 2015 and 2019,<sup><a href="#ref39" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref39">39</a></sup> most likely due
to the regional COVID-19 lockdown during the current study’s
sampling period.</p>
<section id="sec3.1"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Cytotoxicity and Chemical Characterization
of Water-Extracted
PM<sub>2.5</sub>
</h3>
<p>For all 85 filters and 30 field blanks,
the toxicity of aqueous extracts was examined in primary human nasal
epithelial cells (triplicate, 48 h) using fivefold dilutions of the
extracts in the nasal cell culture medium. The median cytotoxicity
for 30 field blanks was low (0.036%) but ranged up to 10.5% in one
batch. To be consistent and conservative, we therefore subtracted
10.5% cytotoxicity from each sample, resulting in 49% of the sample
extracts (<em>n</em> = 42) with cytotoxicity above maximum
blank (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S6</a>). Interestingly, four of
the five most toxic samples were collected in late fall or winter
months (November–December 2020 and November 2019). In preliminary
tests, three random PM<sub>2.5</sub> sample filters were sectioned
and extracted separately by three separate protocols, including the
current aqueous protocol, as well as one with hexane and another with
methanol/toluene. Importantly, only the aqueous extracts produced
measurable cytotoxicity in the current bioassay (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S2</a>); thus, all further characterizations focused on
the aqueous extracts. According to the literature, PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracted using both water-based and polar solvent-based methods
showed toxic effects (on cellular and acellular assays).<sup><a href="#ref40" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref40">40</a>−<a href="#ref42" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref42">42</a></sup> In our samples, the higher cytotoxicity observed with our endpoint
(cytotoxicity to human nasal cells) could be attributed to the extraction
of chemicals that are more likely to cross biological barriers in
human cells.<sup><a href="#ref4" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref4">4</a>,<a href="#ref22" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref22">22</a></sup></p>
<p>The composition of the
inorganic components in the water-extracted samples is presented in <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>, and a detailed discussion
is reported in <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Supporting Results</a> (p. S5).
Further characterization of organic compounds in the aqueous extracts
by nontarget analysis revealed 52,085 molecular features (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S5</a>). The overall complexity can be visualized
in plots of standard mass defect versus molecular mass, including
27,724 molecular features in positive ionization mode (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>A) and 24,361 in negative ionization
mode (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>B). Among
all features, 1034 features were redundant (i.e., ionized in both
modes) based on the criteria of sharing a common precursor ion mass
(±0.001 Da, after accounting for protonation/deprotonation) and
retention time (±0.15 min), resulting in a final total of 51,051
unique molecular features. Previous studies have described similarly
complex mixtures of WSOCs in PM<sub>2.5</sub> from south and east
Asia.<sup><a href="#ref13" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref13">13</a>,<a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> Notably, Papazian et al. also compared the
chemical composition of PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracted by water, polar
solvents, and nonpolar solvents and found that the water-extractable
substances were the most complex mixture.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> Going beyond traditional spectral library screening of detected
features, the next sections describe the nontarget analysis workflows
uniquely developed for the study, and the statistics applied with
all the chemical signals detected (<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">3</a></a>).</p>

</div></div></section><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig2"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/6faa5ab82625/es4c09615_0002.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr2" height="5375" width="3946" alt="Figure 2"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Standard mass defect plots of all nontarget
molecular features
detected in (A) positive ionization (<em>n</em> = 27,724)
and (B) negative ionization (<em>n</em> = 24,361) modes, colored
by retention time. For the combined data sets, the corresponding total
number of features with an assigned formula is shown by (C) heteroatomic
formula class and (D) number of double bond equivalents (DBEs). Note
that the four halogenated formula classes (shown as F, Cl, I, or Br)
contain other heteroatoms and multiple halogens, but for simplicity,
these are grouped.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig3"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/8dc65a215bd6/es4c09615_0003.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr3" height="1422" width="4175" alt="Figure 3"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig3/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Unsupervised multivariate
analyses of the 85 samples using all
chemical signals as variables, first by (A) principal component analysis
(PCA; PC1 = 0.096 and PC2 = 0.083, <em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> =
0.0096) and then by (B) <em>T</em>-distributed stochastic
neighbor embedding (<em>t</em>-sne; perplexity = 2 and 2000
steps). Samples are colored by the average temperature at the observatory
during collection, with red indicating warmer days and blue indicating
cooler days.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3.2"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Nontarget Analysis of Water-Extracted
PM<sub>2.5</sub>
</h3>
<p>Spectral library searches in MassBank Europe
and MoNA resulted in
2637 preliminary spectral matches (similarity score &gt;70%, Δ<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> &lt; 5 ppm). From this list of structural
annotations, 39 authentic standards were obtained and analyzed by
the current analytical method. From the 39 authentic standards, 16
molecular features were confirmed at level 1 confidence (<a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>), considering the
retention index (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S4</a>), MS2 spectrum,
precursor mass, and isotope abundance. As suggested by Alygizakis
et al.,<sup><a href="#ref43" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref43">43</a></sup> details of the molecular confirmations
are shown (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figures S5–S20</a>), and
these included plastic additives (e.g., dibutyl adipate, pentadecanoic
acid), personal care products (e.g., <em>N</em>-dodecanoyl-<em>N</em>-methylglycine, 3,4-dimethoxybenzaldehyde), and pharmaceuticals
(e.g., azelaic acid). Nonconfirmed molecular candidates retained only
their assigned molecular formula (level 4) but not the structural
annotation.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/tbl2/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<div class="tw-foot p"><div class="fn" id="t2fn1">
<sup>a</sup><p class="display-inline">ΔRTI = difference between
feature and reference standard’s RTI. Δ<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> = difference between feature and reference standard’s
precursor mass.</p>
</div></div></section><p>To estimate
the geographic sources of any feature, the normalized
peak area can be multiplied by the proportional weight of each back-trajectory
source region for each sample (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B), and the results are summed over the campaign. Example
results are shown in <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>, including for two level 1 confirmed substances, perfluorooctanoic
acid (PFOA, <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>A) and perfluorononanoic acid (PFNA, <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>B). The results suggest that the primary
geographic source regions for PFOA are northern China (clusters 5
and 6), which together account for 43% of the PFOA signal; this is
notable as these regions’ combined frequency in back-trajectories
was only 28% (i.e., source contributions from regions 5 and 6 both
exceed the red lines in <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>A). In contrast, the primary source region for PFNA was the
East Sea/Sea of Japan (region 1), which accounted for 33% of the PFNA
signal, even though air coming from this region contributed only 16%
of the time overall. These example results are consistent with known
manufacturing sources of these two substances in the region: specifically,
China became the primary global manufacturing site for PFOA after
2002, and Japan was a major historical producer of PFNA.<sup><a href="#ref44" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref44">44</a></sup> Due to their current levels in environmental
samples, extreme environmental persistence, and mobility in the atmosphere,
it has been argued that these two PFASs are planetary boundary threats
(e.g., ref (<a href="#ref45" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref45">45</a>)), yet
the environmental occurrence of most PFAS has not been monitored and
nontarget screening for PFAS may help to prioritize future targeted
studies.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig4"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/66358916b4d7/es4c09615_0004.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr4" height="4820" width="3345" alt="Figure 4"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig4/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Example MS2 spectral library matches (top of each panel) of four
nontarget features compared to the reference spectra of PFOA (A),
PFNA (B), tebupirimfos (C), and dicyclohexylamine (D). Both PFOA (A)
and PFNA (B) were confirmed with authentic standards, level 1 confidence.
The relative source contribution of each back-trajectory cluster region
for each substance (bottom of each panel) is also shown, estimated
by multiplying peak areas with the relative contribution of air coming
from each source region in each sample. The red dashed lines show
the relative frequency (%) that air masses originate from each back-trajectory
region; thus, source contributions above or below these red lines
indicate regions whose air is relatively enriched or depleted in the
substance(s), respectively. The six back-trajectory clusters represent
different source regions for the air reaching the sampling station:
1—East Sea/Sea of Japan; 2—East China Sea; 3—Korean
Peninsula; 4—Shandong/Beijing; 5—North China; 6—North
China/Mongolia.</p></figcaption></figure><p>An additional 28 features were
annotated (level 2 confidence) based
on the combination of high-quality MS2 spectral library match (score
&gt;70%), precursor mass in MS1 (Δ<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> &lt; 5 ppm), and retention index (ΔRTI &lt; 30)
(<a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">Table <a href="#tbl2" class="usa-link">2</a></a> and <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figures S22–S25</a>). RTIs of library matches
were retrieved
from the online Norman Substance Database, and RTIs of the features
were calculated using the RTI calibration solution analyzed with the
samples (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S4</a>). Notable level 2 annotated
features included insecticide tebupirimfos (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>C), which has not previously been reported
in PM<sub>2.5</sub> to our knowledge, and dicyclohexylamine (<a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig4" class="usa-link">4</a></a>D), a tire-derived
substance<sup><a href="#ref46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref46">46</a></sup> that was previously detected
in aqueous extracts of PM<sub>2.5</sub> in South Asia.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> Both compounds had intense signals in days impacted
by region 1 (East Sea/Sea of Japan), and dicyclohexylamine was also
more prominent in days impacted by region 4 (Shandong/Beijing). Aspirin
(acetylsalicylic acid) was also among the level 2 annotations and
interestingly is known to be transformed to one of the confirmed (i.e.,
level 1) compounds, salicylic acid. Although salicylic acid can be
naturally produced by plants, seeing its signal correlated to the
signal of aspirin increases confidence in both annotations and suggests
that the sources of both substances detected here were largely anthropogenic.
Moreover, looking at the normalized peak areas from these two compounds
throughout the current campaign, their quantitative responses were
moderately correlated (Pearson’s <em>R</em> = 0.5, <em>p</em> = 9.6 × 10<sup>–7</sup>, <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S21</a>).</p>
<p>For molecular formula assignment of unknown
features, we developed
a completely new workflow: 50 reference standards (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S2</a>) were first used to validate the performance of two
software tools (SIRIUS and MS-FINDER) with the current data set. These
included 21 standards injected in positive ionization mode and 29
in negative ionization mode, all having a wide range of molecular
mass (121–916 Da), chemical functionalities, and molecular
formulas including various heteroatoms (P, S, N, and O) and halogens
(F, Cl, Br, and I). Considering the top two ranked predicted molecular
formulas for each reference standard, both SIRIUS and MS-FINDER performed
well, with accurate formulas predicted by both tools as the top-ranked
for 76% (38 of 50) of substances (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S2</a>). Moreover, for all 50 reference standards, the accurate formula
was predicted by at least one of the tools, either as top-ranked or
second-ranked. Performance was even better considering the subset
of substances that did not contain a F atom, in which case the accurate
formula was predicted by both tools as the top-ranked for 88% (38
of 43) of reference standards. Given the possibility that other fluorinated
substances may be missed by this workflow, a secondary workflow was
applied to identify highly fluorinated substances.</p>
<p>Based on
excellent performance for most reference standards, we
assigned a molecular formula to unknown features when both SIRIUS
and MS-FINDER predicted the same molecular formula, ranked as the
top 1 or 2. Among all detected molecular features, 13,907 (27%) were
thereby assigned a molecular formula (level 4 confidence<sup><a href="#ref36" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref36">36</a></sup>) by these conservative criteria. Over the whole
campaign, the most commonly detected molecules in the aqueous extracts
of PM<sub>2.5</sub> belonged to simple oxygenated formula classes
(i.e., C<sub><em>x</em></sub>H<sub><em>y</em></sub>O<sub>Z</sub>), dominated by O<sub>4</sub> &gt; O<sub>3</sub> &gt;
O<sub>2</sub> &gt; O<sub>5</sub> in both positive and negative modes
(<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>C). Nevertheless,
more than half of the assigned formulas (55%) contained at least one
nitrogen atom, including prominent classes with mixed nitrogen and
oxygen (e.g., C<sub><em>x</em></sub>H<sub><em>y</em></sub>NO<sub><em>z</em></sub> and C<sub><em>x</em></sub>H<sub><em>y</em></sub>N<sub>2</sub>O<sub><em>z</em></sub>, where <em>z</em> = 1–6). A range of mixed
sulfur- and oxygen-containing molecules, detected almost exclusively
in negative mode (i.e., SO<sub><em>x</em></sub><sup>–</sup>, where <em>x</em> = 3–7, <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>C), were indicative of organic sulfonates
and sulfates. Papazian et al. previously categorized N- and S-containing
molecules as among the molecular hallmarks of polluted air,<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a></sup> and in PM<sub>2.5</sub> collected in Beijing,
Ma et al. reported seasonal trends with higher abundance of organosulfates
in summer and more abundant nitrogenated compounds in winter.<sup><a href="#ref15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref15">15</a></sup> It is only notable here that the current campaign
was biased toward winter months (43 of 85 samples), as the 16 month
period covered two winters (<a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>B). The number of double bond equivalents (DBEs) in
annotated molecules was also calculated from each assigned formula
(i.e., DBE = C + 1 – (H/2) – (<em>X</em>/2)
+ (N/2); <em>X</em> = number of halogens). The distribution
of DBEs in both modes suggested a preponderance of molecules with
at least four DBEs (representing 57% of all formulas), which may therefore
contain aromatic structures but also a large number of nonaromatic
substances (DBEs &lt; 4, representing 43% of all assigned formulas)
and, to a lesser extent, aliphatic structures (DBE = 0, 4% of formulas)
(<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>D). The assigned
formulas for hundreds of substances also contained F (<em>n</em> = 357, including the flagged PFAS described later) or Cl (<em>n</em> = 251) (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>C), notably overcoming a limitation for halogens in previously
described workflows for molecular formula assignments in PM<sub>2.5</sub>.<sup><a href="#ref14" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref14">14</a>,<a href="#ref15" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref15">15</a></sup></p>
<p>This general workflow used here for
molecular formula assignments
was shown to underestimate the number of fluorinated compounds. This
was because the workflow relied on a consensus between MS-FINDER and
SIRIUS, but MS-FINDER performed poorly for all fluorinated reference
standards. Nevertheless, SIRIUS accurately predicted the molecular
formulas (top-ranked) of all six PFAS reference standards in validation
testing (i.e., C<sub>6</sub>HF<sub>11</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, C<sub>7</sub>HF<sub>13</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, C<sub>8</sub>HF<sub>15</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, C<sub>9</sub>HF<sub>17</sub>O<sub>2</sub>, C<sub>4</sub>HF<sub>9</sub>O<sub>3</sub>S, and C<sub>8</sub>H<sub>2</sub>F<sub>17</sub>NO<sub>2</sub>S; <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S2</a>). Thus, a separate
workflow using SIRIUS and MS-DIAL was developed here. Among all unknown
features in the current study, 6122 were initially predicted to contain
at least two fluorine atoms by SIRIUS, thus each plausibly corresponding
to a theoretical PFAS (i.e., containing a −CF<sub>2</sub>–
moiety). In previous work, PFAS have been successfully identified
in water, biota, and particulate matter by manual searches for common
perfluoroalkyl fragments.<sup><a href="#ref46" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref46">46</a>−<a href="#ref50" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref50">50</a></sup> Thus, using the MS/MS fragment searcher tool in MS-DIAL, 13 common
PFAS fragments ([C<sub><em>x</em></sub>F<sub>2<em>x</em>+1</sub>]<sup>−</sup>; <em>x</em> = 2–14, <a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S3</a>) were searched in the MS2 spectra of
all unknown features in negative ionization mode (tolerance of 0.01
Da, ion abundance threshold &gt;10%), and 97 features contained at
least
two common PFAS fragments. Of these, 74 also had a top-ranked molecular
formula corresponding to a PFAS, as predicted by SIRIUS (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_001.xlsx" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Table S4</a>). Some of these corresponded to spectra
of known PFASs (e.g., perfluoroundecanoate, C<sub>10</sub>F<sub>21</sub>CO<sub>2</sub><sup>–</sup>), which were identified by suspect
screening (using spectral libraries), but most corresponded to unknown
PFASs with no spectral match (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>). Together with other recent discoveries, this demonstrates
the prevalence of unexpected and unknown PFASs in our environment.<sup><a href="#ref51" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref51">51</a></sup></p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig5"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/0af1e6730e92/es4c09615_0005.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr5" height="4498" width="4175" alt="Figure 5"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig5/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Deconvoluted MS2 spectra (A–E) of five example
PFASs (out
of 74 total) discovered in aqueous extracts of PM<sub>2.5</sub> by
the workflow incorporating SIRIUS formula prediction and manual MS2
fragment searching. Green spectral fragments are those used by SIRIUS
to assign the molecular formula. (F) Considering all unknown PFAS
features together, the major source region was the Korean Peninsula
(cluster 3), and the least important was the cleanest air coming from
the China Sea (cluster 2). The red dashed lines show the relative
frequency (%) that air masses originate from each back-trajectory
region; thus, source contributions above or below these red lines
indicate regions whose air is relatively enriched or depleted in the
substance(s), respectively. The six back-trajectory clusters represent
different source regions of the air reaching the sampling station:
1—East Sea/Sea of Japan; 2—East China Sea; 3—Korean
Peninsula; 4—Shandong/Beijing; 5—North China; 6—North
China/Mongolia.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Although the exact structures
of these highly fluorinated substances
remain uncertain, the diagnostic perfluoroalkyl fragments confirm
that these are PFASs. Moreover, given that the structures of these
unknown PFASs contain long perfluoroalkyl fragments, all of these
substances are likely environmental precursors to persistent perfluorinated
acids. For example, like perfluoroundecanoate, the spectrum of the
unknown PFAS in <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>D contains a C<sub>10</sub>F<sub>21</sub> moiety. By analogy to the
atmospheric oxidation of 10:2 fluorotelomer alcohol (C<sub>10</sub>F<sub>21</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>CH<sub>2</sub>OH), this unknown substance
could plausibly yield perfluoroundecanoate in the atmosphere through
hydroxyl radical oxidation processes.<sup><a href="#ref52" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref52">52</a></sup> Similarly, the example substances shown are likely precursors to
PFOA (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>B), perfluoropropionate
(<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>C), and perfluoropentanoate
(<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>E) in the
environment.</p>
<p>In general, the source region for these 74 prospective
PFAS was
primarily the Korean Peninsula (region 3), followed by China (regions
4–6), with the least coming from the East China Sea (region
2) (<a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>F). <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig5" class="usa-link">5</a></a>F also shows a smaller
contribution of PFASs from air masses originating from the Sea of
Japan (region 1). This could be due to the documented production of
PFASs in Japan<sup><a href="#ref53" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref53">53</a></sup> and emissions via sea
spray aerosols.<sup><a href="#ref54" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref54">54</a></sup> Overall, these data suggest
that the sources of these unknown PFASs may be widespread and possibly
linked to their contemporary use in several areas of East Asia.</p></section><section id="sec3.3"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Effects of Atmospheric Conditions and Origin of the Air on Chemistry</h3>
<p>To broadly examine for relationships between chemistry, back-trajectories,
and ambient temperature, all chemistry data (inorganic and all WSOC
nontarget features) were combined in unsupervised multivariate models.
An initial PCA analysis showed no strong grouping of the samples (<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">3</a></a>A), with PC1 and
PC2 each explaining less than 10% of the overall variability, and <em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> was lower than 0.01. However, by coloring
the samples by average temperature during collection, it was evident
that PC1 (9.6% of variation) partially separated the samples, with
samples collected on warmer days grouping negative on PC1, while those
from cooler days grouping positive on PC1. The potential influence
of each back-trajectory source region on the detected chemistry was
similarly investigated, but no separation of the samples was found
(data not shown). In a follow-up <em>t</em>-sne analysis—an
alternative unsupervised model with the potential to detect groupings
based on smaller variations in the data set<sup><a href="#ref55" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref55">55</a></sup>—more distinctive grouping of the samples was achieved; again,
the grouping was to some extent correlated with average temperature
(<a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig3" class="usa-link">3</a></a>B), indicating
that the grouping observed with the <em>t</em>-sne analysis
is due to a subset of variables among all of the chemical signals
detected. The same <em>t</em>-sne output was replotted by
sizing each sample marker by the relative contribution of the two
maritime regions (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3A</a>), the Korean
Peninsula (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3B</a>), Shandong/Beijing
(<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3C</a>), or the two North China source
regions (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3D</a>). These combined results
suggested that variations in the complex chemistry could be attributed
to the combination of warmer temperatures and maritime source regions
in the summer (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3A</a>) and cooler temperatures
and northern China source regions in the winter (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3D</a>).</p>
<p>It was not possible in this study to disentangle
the independent influences of temperature and back-trajectory factors
on sample chemistry because both factors are correlated and influenced
by season (e.g., in summer, the air is warmer and mainly comes from
maritime regions, while in winter, the air is cooler and often comes
from China, <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig1" class="usa-link">1</a></a>). Nevertheless, in the next sections, we applied supervised multivariate
models to discriminate which chemicals are most influenced by warm
versus cool temperatures or maritime versus continental back-trajectories.</p>
<p>To determine which organic or inorganic substances (<em>X</em> variables) were most associated with temperature, an OPLS-DA model
was built, with each sample classified according to temperature (<em>Y</em> variable). The average temperature across the campaign
was 15.0 °C; thus, samples were dichotomized as either higher
temperature (average temperature during collection &gt;15 °C, <em>n</em> = 42) or lower temperature (average temperature &lt;15
°C, <em>n</em> = 45). The OPLS-DA model output generally
separated samples by temperature (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a></a>A), with a good fit to the data (<em>R</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.733) and acceptable robustness (<em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.472). Kim et al. described a change in
the optical properties of PM<sub>2.5</sub> at the current site in
winter (with the highest aerosol scattering and second-highest aerosol
absorption attributed to winter months), suggesting a different chemical
composition of aerosols in winter compared to other months.<sup><a href="#ref24" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref24">24</a></sup> Interestingly, one particular sample from higher
temperature collection days was grouped with samples from lower temperature
days, but this unique sample was taken during an unusually warm period
in winter (average temperature 16.0 °C, December 6–8,
2020), suggesting that solar intensity (together with temperature)
could be an underlying factor in the chemistry of PM<sub>2.5</sub>. Photochemical oxidation of water-soluble brown carbon was recently
demonstrated during atmospheric transport of PM<sub>2.5</sub> from
the Indian subcontinent to an Indian Ocean receptor site.<sup><a href="#ref37" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref37">37</a></sup></p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig6"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/d7d1adfcebd4/es4c09615_0006.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr6" height="2852" width="4175" alt="Figure 6"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig6/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>(A, B) Output of an OPLS-DA model contrasting all of the
chemical
signals (52,113 variables) measured in PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracts (<em>n</em> = 85) taken on high average temperature days (yellow)
and low average temperature days (teal), including (A) score plot
showing separation of the samples (<em>R</em><sup>2</sup> =
0.733 and <em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.472) and (B) scatter
plot of the variable’s influence on projection (VIP) score
versus correlation coefficients, pCorr, for all nontarget features.
The subset of features with VIP score &gt;1.5 and |pCorr| &gt; 0.5
are highlighted,
corresponding to features most correlated with warmer (high <em>T</em>; pCorr &gt;0.5) or colder (low <em>T</em>; pCorr
&lt;0.5) temperatures. Molecular characterization of high-<em>T</em> and low-<em>T</em> substances are contrasted by
distributions of (C) double bond equivalents (DBEs) in bar charts
and (D) assigned molecular formula classes in pie charts.</p></figcaption></figure><p>Among all chemical variables, none of the metals or major
ions
were highly correlated with temperature. However, 2221 nontarget features
were highly correlated with a lower temperature (11 annotated at level
2), while only 288 features (1 annotated at level 2) were highly correlated
with a higher temperature. Molecular formulas were confidently assigned
to approximately half of the highly correlated features; specifically,
53% of features correlated with lower temperatures, while 47% of features
correlated with higher temperatures. Among this subset of molecules,
those correlating with lower temperatures had significantly higher
DBEs (mean DBE = 5.5) than those correlating with higher-temperature
days (mean DBE = 3.6; <em>p</em> = 0.000011, <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a></a>C). To some extent, these differences
may relate to seasonal shifts in back-trajectory sources of the air
because more polluted continental air arrives at the observatory on
cooler days than on warmer days. Nevertheless, this may also indicate
that more aromatic and cyclic structures are present on colder days
when photooxidation potential is lower, consistent with the fact that
highly oxygenated compounds (i.e., O<sub>6</sub>–O<sub>9</sub>) were highly correlated with warmer temperatures (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a></a>D). As a simple analogy, the
photooxidation of benzene (DBE = 4) is initiated by hydroxyl radical
and proceeds through ring-opening to form oxidized molecules with
lower DBEs, e.g., butanediol (DBE = 3, O<sub>2</sub>), 2,3-epoxy-butandiol
(DBE = 3, O<sub>3</sub>), and glyoxal (DBE = 1, O<sub>2</sub>).<sup><a href="#ref56" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref56">56</a></sup> To summarize, it is likely that seasonal changes
in photooxidation and back-trajectory source regions together contribute
to the observed differences in PM<sub>2.5</sub> chemistry on cooler
and warmer days.</p>
<p>The <em>t</em>-sne analysis also suggested
that the chemistry
of the aqueous PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracts was different when influenced
by the two maritime back-trajectory source regions (<a href="https://pubs.acs.org/doi/suppl/10.1021/acs.est.4c09615/suppl_file/es4c09615_si_002.pdf" class="usa-link usa-link--external" data-ga-action="click_feat_suppl" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Figure S3A</a>). Thus, in a separate OPLS-DA model (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a></a>) using the same chemical <em>X</em> variables, we dichotomized the samples according to whether
they were predominantly influenced by the combination of maritime
back-trajectory regions (<em>n</em> = 21 samples) or the four
continental regions (<em>n</em> = 64). The resulting OPLS-DA
model was robust (<em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.37) and explained
a high proportion of the variation (<em>R</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.721). Of the 2157 variables strongly correlating with maritime
source regions, all were nontarget features, including one identified
at level 1 by reference standard injection (3,4-dimethoxybenzaldehyde)
and two others annotated at level 2 (MS2 similarity &gt;70%, Δ<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> &lt; 5 ppm, and ΔRTI &lt; 30),
specifically 2-phenylglycine and acetylsalicylic acid (i.e., aspirin).
Aspirin (pharmaceutical) and 3,4-dimethoxybenzaldehyde (used in personal
care products) have anthropogenic sources and are likely released
through wastewater to the coastal sea environments. Thus, sea spray
aerosol generation might explain their presence in PM<sub>2.5</sub>, as described by Johansson et al. for certain PFASs.<sup><a href="#ref57" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref57">57</a></sup> Interestingly, 2-phenylglycine was also correlated
with high-temperature days, and to our knowledge, this is the first
time its presence in ambient air has been reported.</p>
<p>Among features
correlating with the maritime source regions, 55%
had a confidently assigned molecular formula (<a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">7</a></a>C,D). Among these molecules, those correlating
with maritime regions were dominated by saturated compounds (i.e.,
DBE = 0) and lower numbers of DBEs (i.e., 2 &lt; DBE &lt; 6, <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">7</a></a>C), which is lower
than the overall DBE distribution of nontarget features (<a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig2" class="usa-link">2</a></a>C) and lower than
the DBE distributions for features correlated with both high and low
temperatures (<a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig6" class="usa-link">6</a></a>C). The predominant formula classes among features highly correlated
with maritime regions were low to moderately oxygenated compounds
(i.e., O<sub>2</sub>–O<sub>5</sub>), followed by nitrogen-containing
oxygenated substances (NO–NO<sub>3</sub>, <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">Figure <a href="#fig7" class="usa-link">7</a></a>D). It has been reported that
commercial shipping (i.e., cargo and tanker ships) can emit nitrogenated
organic chemicals,<sup><a href="#ref58" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref58">58</a>,<a href="#ref59" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref59">59</a></sup> and numerous shipping routes
pass in the vicinity of Jeju Island.<sup><a href="#ref60" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref60">60</a></sup></p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="fig7"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><img class="graphic" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/5670/11823462/1f7f474b0d8b/es4c09615_0007.jpg" loading="lazy" id="gr7" height="2777" width="4175" alt="Figure 7"></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/fig7/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Output
of an OPLS-DA model contrasting the WSOC profiles of all
PM<sub>2.5</sub> extracts (<em>n</em> = 85) impacted primarily
from maritime regions (regions 1 and 2) to those impacted primarily
from continental source regions (regions 3–6), including the
(A) score plot showing separation of the samples (1 + 1, <em>R</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.721 and <em>Q</em><sup>2</sup> = 0.37) and
(B) scatter plot of the variable’s influence on projection
(VIP) score versus correlation coefficients, pCorr, for all nontarget
features. Nontarget features with pCorr &gt;0.5 and VIP &gt; 1.5 are
highlighted
in (B), corresponding to those molecules most positively correlated
with the maritime source regions whose molecular characteristics are
shown by distributions of (C) double bond equivalents (DBEs) in a
bar chart and (D) molecular formula class distributions.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec3.4"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">Limitations and Future Directions</h3>
<p>The present study
confirms that the cytotoxic water-soluble fraction of PM<sub>2.5</sub> has a complex chemical composition that is seasonally influenced
by regional meteorology and shifting back-trajectories. This seasonal
variability was primarily in the complex profiles of water-soluble
organic compounds, and a robust workflow for molecular formula assignment
and novel molecular discovery was effectively demonstrated. For research
to progress on the molecular drivers of PM<sub>2.5</sub> toxicity,
the current results emphasize that detailed PM<sub>2.5</sub> chemical
characterization should be conducted concurrently with toxicological
testing, as studies relying only on total PM<sub>2.5</sub> concentrations
will be blind to the underlying variability and specific chemical
differences in their test materials. Considering that half of the
current extracts (<em>n</em> = 42) were cytotoxic to human
nasal cells, further research is needed to identify the key chemical
drivers of toxicity in aqueous extracts of PM<sub>2.5</sub> by applying
comprehensive effects-based methods<sup><a href="#ref61" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref61">61</a>,<a href="#ref62" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="ref62">62</a></sup> in other relevant
human cells (e.g., lung cells) with more detailed mechanistic or molecular-level
endpoints (e.g., for oxidative stress).</p>
<p>It is also important
to note that the start of the current sampling campaign, in late 2019,
approximately coincided with the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic, which
spread globally by early 2020. Therefore, the majority of our sampling
period was during the pandemic when neighboring countries underwent
different extents of lockdown, which likely influenced the current
results. As discussed, PM<sub>2.5</sub> concentrations during the
current campaign were lower than what was previously reported at this
site, and it is likely that the chemistry of the PM<sub>2.5</sub> was
also influenced by diminished industrial activity and major societal
changes in east Asia. Nevertheless, samples were still shown to be
toxic to human nasal cells, in particular the complex mixture of substances
present in the water-soluble fraction.</p></section></section><section id="ack1" class="ack"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">Acknowledgments</h2>
